### CVE-2026-134056 - Out-of-bounds write in padded block cipher decryption in AWS-LC
- Source: AWS Security Bulletins | Published: 2026-11-23T20:29:41Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-134056 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: Bulletin ID: 2026-196-AWS Publication Date: 11/23/2026 12:00 PM PST Description: We identified CVE-2026-134056, an out-of-bounds write issue in AWS-LC's padded block cipher decryption. It affects applications that decrypt CBC or ECB ciphertext with PKCS7 padding over multiple update calls. If the output buffer is the minimum documented size, AWS-LC can write one byte past its end. This might crash the application or corrupt adjacent memory. This issue does not disclose data. The aws-lc-rs StreamingDecryptingKey API has a similar issue with cbc_pkcs7 and ecb_pkcs7. Its less_safe_update function can write up to one block (16 bytes for AES) past the buffer. Single-call decryption is not affected. Neither is decryption without padding, nor modes such as CTR, CFB, and GCM. Customers of AWS services do not need to take action. Refer to the article for the most up-to-date and complete informati
- Technical details: Bulletin ID: 2026-196-AWS Scope: AWS Content Type: Important (requires attention) Publication Date: 11/23/2026 12:00 PM PST

AWS-LC is an open-source, general-purpose cryptographic library.

We identified CVE-2026-134056, an out-of-bounds write issue in AWS-LC's padded block cipher decryption. It affects applications that decrypt CBC or ECB ciphertext with PKCS7 padding over multiple update calls. If the output buffer is the minimum documented size, AWS-LC can write one byte past its end. This might crash the application or corrupt adjacent memory. This issue does not disclose data. The aws-lc-rs StreamingDecryptingKey API has a similar issue with cbc_pkcs7 and ecb_pkcs7. Its less_safe_update function can write up to one block (16 bytes for AES) past the buffer.

Single-call decryption is not affected. Neither is decryption without padding, nor modes such as CTR, CFB, and GCM.

AWS-LC-FIPS 1.x is affected and reached end of support in October 2026.

This issue has been addressed in the following versions:

We recommend upgrading to the latest version and ensuring any forked or derivative code is patched to incorporate the new fixes.

Applications are not affected if they decrypt in a single update call. They are also not affected if the output buffer is at least the input length plus one block. Otherwise, there is no workaround. Upgrade to a fixed version. AWS-LC-FIPS 1.x reached end of support in October 2026 and will not receive a fix. Upgrade to a supported, fixed AWS-LC-FIPS release.

We would like to thank dywzju09 for collaborating on this issue through the coordinated vulnerability disclosure process.

Please email aws-security@amazon.com with any security questions or concerns.
- Fix / remediation: Upgrade to a supported, fixed AWS-LC-FIPS release. Applications using AWS-LC should upgrade to the most recent release of AWS-LC. Otherwise, there is no workaround and applications using AWS-LC should upgrade to the most recent releases of AWS-LC.
- EPSS context: No EPSS score returned — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

SQLite is a C library that implements an SQL database engine. A large subset of SQL92 is supported. A complete database is stored in a single disk file. The API is designed for convenience and ease of use. Applications that link against SQLite can enjoy the power and flexibility of an SQL database without the administrative hassles of supporting a separate database server.

sqlite: sqlite: Arbitrary command execution via .recover command (CVE-2026-119586)

sqlite: sqlite: Denial of service via heap-based buffer overflow in FTS5 (CVE-2026-119373)

sqlite: sqlite: Denial of Service via stack exhaustion from deeply nested Common Table Expressions (CVE-2026-119371)

sqlite: sqlite: Denial of Service via use-after-free in full-text search (CVE-2026-119372)

### OpenRouter drops AI providers over leaked prompts and secrets
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-23T20:00:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: ai_threat
- Summary: OpenRouter removed three AI providers from its platform after discovering that they had violated their stated data-handling policies and exposed sensitive data. [...]
- Technical details: OpenRouter removed three AI providers from its platform after discovering that they had violated their stated data-handling policies and exposed sensitive data.

The company also revoked Zero Data Retention (ZDR) eligibility for 125 endpoints as a precaution and notified all potentially affected customers.

OpenRouter is an AI model broker that lets developers access AI models from different companies using a single API key.

When an app sends a request, OpenRouter forwards it to a provider that runs the selected model and passes the answer back.

Operational technology security company Dragos alerted OpenRouter after sending two million test prompts through the platform, each containing a unique tracking link or decoy credential, and detecting "activity indicating that data from at least three AI providers was exposed."

Dragos discovered that, in some cases, those credentials were leaked to outsiders and used in the wild, indicating that the test prompts had escaped.

“We observed activity indicating that data from at least three AI providers was exposed. Our seeded test prompts were used in attempts to access our controlled test systems and canary AWS accounts,” Dragos says .

OpenRouter says there was no breach on its own systems, making the leak point at the provider level and leading to the decision to remove the three leaking inference providers: Together AI, Phala, and Novita.

Another provider, Atlas Cloud, said it would voluntarily withdraw its models from OpenRouter pending security testing, following a third-party security incident.

The other three published separate announcements responding to Dragos’ findings and explaining how the leaks occurred.

Together AI said an AI coding agent exposed a repository credential on GitHub, allowing an unauthorized party to download stored prompt samples from June and July involving MiniMax M3, GLM-5.2, and Kimi K2.6.

Phala said an attacker compromised a server processing DeepSeek V4 Flash 0731 requests routed through OpenRouter between October 21 and 22, and possibly up to November 8, and obtained application credentials from affected virtual machines.

Novita said a routing misconfiguration forwarded some MiniMax requests, including those sent through OpenRouter, to MiniMax’s official API between May 28 and November 11.

All four providers are tightening security controls, strengthening their zero data retention or data deletion policies, and advising potentially impacted users to rotate any secrets that may have been exposed in the leaked requests.

OpenRouter is also adapting Dragos’ tests for ongoing use to check whether AI providers forward or leak user requests/responses to third parties, and will require validation before restoring ZDR eligibility.

### Microsoft Azure flaw exploited to steal customers' API credentials
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-23T19:42:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: identity
- Summary: Microsoft is warning customers that a security vulnerability in Azure API Management (APIM) Classic tiers was exploited to steal stored API credentials, with Norwegian authorities linking the flaw to cyberattacks against energy companies. [...]
- Technical details: Microsoft is warning customers that a security vulnerability in Azure API Management (APIM) Classic tiers was exploited to steal stored API credentials, with Norwegian authorities linking the flaw to cyberattacks against energy companies.

Azure API Management is a cloud service that lets organizations publish, secure, monitor, and manage APIs that connect applications, services, and data.

In an Azure Service Health security advisory (Tracking ID: MZK9-GH0) sent to customers and shared with BleepingComputer, Microsoft said it identified the vulnerability on November 18, 2026, and deployed a mitigation the following day.

"Our investigation found that customers were exposed and an unauthorized party could have used the issue to access secrets stored in Azure API Management Classic instances before Microsoft identified the security issue," Microsoft said in the advisory.

Microsoft told BleepingComputer that the vulnerability impacted the Developer, Basic, Standard, and Premium tiers of Azure API Management (APIM) Classic, but that the Consumption tier of APIM Classic and all APIM v2 tiers were not affected.

According to Microsoft, the vulnerability allowed unauthorized access to certain management functionality when APIM Classic instances were not configured according to recommended security best practices.

This access could have allowed attackers to perform administrative operations, including accessing secrets stored in the service.

Microsoft said the following information may have been exposed:

Client secrets associated with OpenID Connect providers

These credentials could potentially allow attackers to access additional applications, services, or backend systems, depending on their permissions.

Microsoft said it closed the unauthorized access path on November 19 and rotated Microsoft-controlled keys and credentials where appropriate.

However, the company warned that its remediation would not invalidate customer credentials that may have been compromised before the fix was deployed.

"This remediation blocks the unauthorized management-plane access path that could allow an actor to perform administrative operations, including secrets management, against Azure API Management services," Microsoft warned.

"However, it does not automatically invalidate credentials that may have been accessed before the fix was deployed."

The vulnerability affects Azure API Management Classic tiers, while Microsoft said APIM v2 tiers are not impacted.

Microsoft urged affected customers to immediately rotate potentially exposed credentials, investigate suspicious activity in systems accessible through those credentials, and review available logs for signs of unauthorized access.

In a Reddit post , an administrator claimed their organization discovered the vulnerability after detecting unauthorized access to Azure Key Vault using a valid administrator token.

### Spanish LaLiga confirms hackers stole soccer fans’ personal data
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-23T18:38:40Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: LaLiga, Spain's top men's football league, disclosed a data breach that exposed personal information belonging to users of its services and those of some affiliated clubs. [...]
- Technical details: LaLiga, Spain's top men's football league, disclosed a data breach that exposed personal information belonging to users of its services and those of some affiliated clubs.

LaLiga, Spain's top men's professional football league, disclosed a data breach that exposed personal information belonging to users of its services and those of some affiliated clubs.

According to the organization, the incident occurred between November 14 and 16, and the attacker extracted data from compromised systems.

LaLiga organizes Spain’s top two men’s professional football divisions.

The top league is one of the world’s most prominent football leagues, where clubs with a global audience, such as Real Madrid, FC Barcelona, and Atlético Madrid, play.

On November 16, the organization’s security team identified the source of the unauthorized access and implemented measures to block the attack and stop the data extraction.

“LALIGA was the target of a cyberattack on one of its technology platforms for fan services,” reads the announcement .

“Between November 14 and 16, a cybercriminal gained unauthorized access to the platform and extracted personal data belonging to users registered for digital services offered by LALIGA and some clubs.”

LaLiga underlined that the incident was confined to a specific platform and did not extend to other systems that support the running of the competitions.

After implementing containment measures, LaLiga launched an investigation to determine the scope of the incident and introduced more thorough monitoring to detect future attempts to breach its systems.

The football organizer also reported the incident to the Spanish Data Protection Agency (AEPD) and filed a complaint with the National Police’s Central Cybercrime Unit.

Impacted clubs were also directly notified, and actions to inform impacted fans are being coordinated.

At the time of writing, no ransomware group has claimed responsibility for the attack on LaLiga.

BleepingComputer has contacted the organization to learn more about the attack and which clubs were impacted, but we have not received a response as of publication.

### Webworm Suspected of Modifying SoftEther VPN to Evade Detection and Hide Remote Access
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-23T17:45:15Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: apt_intrusion, edge_vpn
- Summary: The China-aligned threat actor known as Webworm is suspected to be using a custom version of the open-source SoftEther VPN Bridge tool in an attempt to remove recognizable detection fingerprints, while disguising it as network-monitoring software to establish covert remote access to compromised networks. The modified variant "implements various tricks to hide itself from security tools,"
- Technical details: Rapid7 links a stealth-focused SoftEther VPN Bridge variant to Webworm with moderate confidence, detailing changes designed to evade detection.

The China-aligned threat actor known as Webworm is suspected to be using a custom version of the open-source SoftEther VPN Bridge tool in an attempt to remove recognizable detection fingerprints, while disguising it as network-monitoring software to establish covert remote access to compromised networks.

The modified variant "implements various tricks to hide itself from security tools," Rapid7 said in an analysis, attributing it to the adversarial collective with moderate confidence.

First publicly detailed by Broadcom-owned Symantec in September 2022, Webworm is the name assigned to a China-aligned nation-state group that has targeted government entities, IT services, and electric power companies across Russia, Georgia, Mongolia, and several other Asian nations.

One of the tools used by the threat actor is a legitimate software called SoftEther VPN , with the bridge acting as a pathway connecting the compromised network and the attacker-controlled infrastructure.

The latest findings from Rapid7 revolve around a Windows executable (" netcapa.exe "), which is a modified build of SoftEther VPN Bridge ("vpnbridge_x64.exe") version 4.44, build 9807. The mutated version, rebranded as NtMonitor VPN, masquerades itself as Network Monitor Software from a company called Network Systems Solutions Inc. The artifact was uploaded to VirusTotal around mid-January 2026.

"The PDB path included in the executable reads C:\Users\Administrator\Desktop\SoftEtherVPN_Stable\SoftEtherVPN_Stable\SoftEtherVPN_Stable\src\DebugFiles\pdb\x64_Release\vpnbridge.pdb," the cybersecurity company said. "As such, the tool doesn't hide its identity from reverse engineers, only external interactions."

The renaming is also said to be inconsistent, with many registry key paths still referencing the program as "SoftEther Project," even though a number of other occurrences of "SoftEther VPN" have been replaced by "NtMonitor VPN."

One of the most noticeable changes identified in the altered version is the use of innocuous-looking HTTP endpoints "/update/alive.cgi" and "/update/picture.cgi" to replace "/vpnsvc/vpn.cgi" and "/vpnsvc/connect.cgi," respectively, so that they look like ordinary web application activity.

It also changes the protocol watermark used by SoftEther VPN as part of the handshake process when initiating communication to help the tool distinguish protocol traffic from ordinary HTTP requests. When the connecting client is found to speak the expected protocol, it proceeds with the handshake. If the check fails, the server returns a decoy HTTP response.

The modified version swaps the GIF-based watermark used by the stock SoftEther VPN utility for a PNG-based watermark so as to bypass existing detection signatures based on the GIF watermark.

Some of the other changes introduced to the program are listed below -

Reducing a resource archive named hamcore.se2 used by SoftEther VPN to its bare minimum, as it's required to be present at runtime, and embedding it directly into the executable, causing the binary's size to increase by about 1.5 MB.

Changing the name of the configuration file from "vpn_bridge.config" to "setting.conf" and making its contents encrypted with a custom algorithm. The encrypted configuration starts with the magic bytes "RCEN" in ASCII.

What's interesting here is that the attackers have not been found to introduce any new functionality to the tool, indicating the primary focus was on stealth.

"It seems like they methodically analyzed SoftEther's detection surface and implemented counter-measures to all of the easy ways a detection engineer may target it," Rapid7 said, calling the latest evolution a hybrid approach that brings together the attacker's use of custom malware and dual-use tools.

"We are observing a sort of hybrid of the two — stock SoftEther software that is not trojanized, but rebuilt from source in a way that erases much of its detection fingerprint."
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

### GlobeTopper loses $2.5 million after hackers steal $70k in gift cards
- Source: BleepingComputer | Published: 2026-11-23T16:50:01Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: none extracted | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: A cyberattack disrupted operations at B2B top-up solutions provider GlobeTopper, causing an estimated $2.5 million in lost revenue, while the attacker redeemed digital gift cards worth $70,000. [...]
- Technical details: A cyberattack disrupted operations at B2B top-up solutions provider GlobeTopper, causing an estimated $2.5 million in lost revenue, while the attacker redeemed digital gift cards worth $70,000.

Parent company iQSTEL said that the intrusion was detected on October 22 and GlobeTopper responded by suspending its platform and starting an investigation.

GlobeTopper is a business-to-business digital gift card distributor. Various retailers, fintech apps, and rewards platforms use its API. According to the platform’s website , it is used by more than 4,000 brands across 65 countries, including IKEA, Adidas, Spotify, Target, and Roblox.

In a filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), iQSTEL said that GlobeTopper detected more unauthorized activity the day after it identified the attack. In response, the company "took the platform and all affected servers offline" in the early hours of October 23 to investigate and fix the problem.

The company deployed a completely rebuilt platform on AWS on October 31, while commercial operations were restored in stages, starting November 1.

GlobeTopper says the attacker obtained certain client API credentials and integration keys, which have been revoked and replaced, and it has observed no unauthorized use of them yet.

“During its investigation, the Company determined that the unauthorized party obtained API credentials and integration keys of certain GlobeTopper business customers,” iQSTEL says .

“All affected credentials and integration keys have been revoked and rotated, and affected business customers have been issued new credentials subject to additional access restrictions.”

The company further stated that an investigation is ongoing to determine whether other customer, personal, payment, or provider data was accessed or acquired.

Regarding the digital gift cards the attackers redeemed, the company says it cannot block, recover, or reclaim them, and will incur the losses.

iQSTEL said the incident hurt GlobeTopper’s fourth-quarter transaction volume and operating results, but as of the filing, it has not determined it to be material.

BleepingComputer has emailed iQSTEL with additional questions regarding the incident, but the company declined to share further information.

At the time of writing, no ransomware groups have listed iQSTEL or GlobeTopper as victims on their extortion portals.

FreeRDP is a free implementation of the Remote Desktop Protocol (RDP), released under the Apache license. The xfreerdp client can connect to RDP servers such as Microsoft Windows machines, xrdp, and VirtualBox.

### UK cyber agency seeing ‘marked rise’ in AI-driven attacks
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T13:45:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Britain’s National Cyber Security Centre said it has observed “a marked rise” since September in cyberattacks assisted by artificial intelligence and warned the technology was increasing attackers’ speed, scale and persistence.
- Business background / implications: Britain’s National Cyber Security Centre said it has observed “a marked rise” since September in cyberattacks assisted by artificial intelligence and warned the technology was increasing attackers’ speed, scale and persistence.

Britain’s National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) said Tuesday it has observed “a marked rise” since September in cyberattacks assisted by artificial intelligence.

Echoing a message the agency has repeatedly stressed, the issue is not that AI is making established cybersecurity practices obsolete, but its adoption by attackers is reducing the margin for error among organizations that are failing to do the basics.

In a blog post , the NCSC warned the technology was increasing attackers’ speed, scale and persistence while also reducing their costs — largely due to the widespread availability of the hacking frameworks “combined with cheap, capable open models or illegal API resellers.”

“Organisations across multiple sectors are reporting incidents as a result of threat actors using ‘agentic offensive frameworks’,” said the NCSC, referring to the software “originally designed for red teaming being applied beyond its defensive purpose.”

The agency said it was “worth noting” that “none of the weaknesses being exploited are new or novel” and that the tools being used by these AI agents “provide no new capability the typical threat actor would have access to.”

The post — co-authored by David Chismon, the NCSC’s chief technology officer for architecture, alongside incident handler Kris B and lead data scientist Ellen T — identified Artex as the predominant framework receiving attention at the moment.

Artex was reportedly behind an incident compromising the European Commission last month, with the FBI warning that it was being used both by criminals and state-sponsored groups.

Naming five other frameworks — Cairn, PentAGI, RedAmon, Xalgorix and Darkmoon — the NCSC said “different tools come and go,” but that the important lesson was to recognize how they all contributed to “an uplift in capability.”

Compared with human attackers without those tools, the agentic frameworks were credited with working more quickly, being exhaustive in their searches, instantly learning from their failures and being cheap to run, at scale.

### McDonald’s Japan discloses breach exposing customer purchase and location data
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T13:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: McDonald’s Japan disclosed a cybersecurity breach that exposed customers’ purchase histories and mobile device location data.
- Business background / implications: McDonald’s Japan disclosed a cybersecurity breach that exposed customers’ purchase histories and mobile device location data.

McDonald’s Japan recently experienced a cybersecurity breach that exposed customers’ purchase histories and mobile device location data.

The fast-food giant said Friday that an unauthorized third party had gained access to a database associated with its official mobile app. The company has not disclosed how many customers were affected, when the intrusion occurred or who was behind it.

The compromised information included customers’ purchase histories, IP addresses, internal customer identification numbers and location data from mobile devices used to access McDonald’s services.

The company said the internal identification numbers could not be used on their own to identify individual customers. Names, email addresses, phone numbers, home addresses and credit card information were not affected by the breach.

McDonald’s Japan said it was working to invalidate the authentication credentials used in the attack but did not explain how the hacker obtained them.

The company has not detected any unauthorized use of its app or related services as a result of the incident and said customers did not need to change their passwords or take other security measures.

McDonald’s Japan is investigating the affected systems and has reported the incident to Japan’s data protection authorities. It remains unclear whether the attacker stole any customer information.

The McDonald’s Japan breach follows other cybersecurity incidents that have affected Japan’s food and retail industries in recent months, disrupting supply chains and forcing some restaurants to scale back operations.

In July, a cyberattack on Japan’s largest refrigerated logistics company disrupted deliveries to food manufacturers, supermarkets and restaurant chains across the country.

Among those affected was KFC Japan, which reported ingredient shortages affecting products including its signature Original Recipe chicken. The disruptions forced some of the chain’s more than 1,300 restaurants to offer limited menus and shorten their operating hours.

Japanese takeout chain Hotto Motto, restaurant chain Yayoi Ken and conveyor-belt sushi operator Kura Sushi were also affected.

The ransomware group RansomHouse claimed responsibility for the attack on the logistics company. The group previously targeted Japanese online retailer Askul, disrupting its e-commerce operations.

### Tomorrow’s quantum threat is today’s open door
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-24T11:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Post-quantum cryptography deserves a place on the roadmap. But in operational technology, the threats doing damage today are far more basic, and far more urgent. The post Tomorrow’s quantum threat is today’s open door appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: OT leaders should prioritize network segmentation, asset inventory, and basic security controls before chasing post-quantum cryptography.

Earlier this month, attackers switched refrigeration systems in supermarkets across nine European countries into defrost mode . Stores were forced to throw out spoiled stock, and as is now being reported, some blood banks in France were hit as well. Around the same time, U.S. agencies including the FBI and CISA warned that internet-exposed refrigeration and temperature controllers were being actively targeted.

The attackers didn’t get in by breaking encryption or cracking passwords. They found admin interfaces exposed to the internet that never should have been public facing, along with known vulnerabilities and, in some cases, default credentials. In other words, unnecessary attack surfaces, unpatched systems, and factory default passwords. They didn’t need a quantum cryptography breakthrough. They had an open door.

That’s worth keeping in mind as the post-quantum cryptography (PQC) conversation picks up speed. New algorithms have been standardized , governments are setting migration timelines , and vendors are urging organizations to start migrating. Quantum computing’s potential to break widely used encryption deserves serious preparation. But nearly all of that guidance is targeted at IT. In operational technology (OT), the PQC timeline looks very different, and the risks hurting organizations today are frankly much lower-hanging fruit.

Before worrying about tomorrow’s lockpick technology, lock today’s open doors.

The devices that run water, electric, critical infrastructure, and manufacturing environments were built for a different era and a different purpose. Some examples are PLCs (programmable logic controllers), HMIs (human-machine interfaces) and OITs (operator interface terminals), as well as RTUs (remote terminal units) and PMRs (pole-mounted routers) in the electric sector.

Start with lifecycles. NIST’s “Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security” (SP 800-82 Rev. 3) notes that OT technology often remains deployed for 10 to 15 years, and sometimes longer. In practice, it’s often much longer. Organizations don’t always inventory these devices, or even realize they’re on the network, so they can sit in public-sector environments for 20 years or more. I’m a millennial, and I’ve assessed equipment at an electrical site that was last updated before I was born.

Then there’s the hardware factor. OT devices

- Evidence: [Original report](https://cyberscoop.com/ot-security-post-quantum-cryptography-op-ed/)

### LaLiga hacked: Spain’s top football league confirms theft of fans’ personal data
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T00:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Spain’s professional soccer league LaLiga said Monday hackers stole personal information belonging to fans in a cyberattack affecting its digital services and those of several Spanish clubs.
- Business background / implications: Spain’s professional soccer league LaLiga said Monday hackers stole personal information belonging to fans in a cyberattack affecting its digital services and those of several Spanish clubs.

The intrusion occurred between November 14 and 16 and targeted a technology platform that provides digital services to fans, LaLiga said in a statemen . The organization has since contained the attack but is still investigating the extent of the breach.

LaLiga represents 42 clubs across Spain’s top two professional men’s soccer divisions. In addition to organizing competitions and managing broadcasting rights, it operates several digital platforms and services for clubs and their fans.

LaLiga said the incident did not disrupt soccer competitions or affect its other systems. Its security teams identified the source of the unauthorized access, stopped the hacker from stealing more data and blocked access to the platform. The league has not disclosed how the hacker broke in or how many fans were affected.

Spain’s National Police is investigating the breach, and LaLiga has notified the country’s data protection regulator.

At least five Spanish soccer clubs — CD Eldense , Real Oviedo , Real Zaragoza , Cordoba CF and CD Leganes — have confirmed that some of their fans’ personal information was compromised.

CD Eldense said the stolen information included personal identification and contact details that fans had provided when registering for digital services. Based on the information available so far, passwords, banking details and payment information were not compromised.

Real Oviedo and Real Zaragoza said their own technology infrastructure and databases had not been affected, while Córdoba CF said the breach involved a platform operated independently of the club.

CD Leganes said it was not aware of any publication or fraudulent use of the stolen information at the time of its announcement.

Spain’s two most prominent soccer clubs, Real Madrid and FC Barcelona, have not publicly said whether their fans were affected.

The affected clubs warned that cybercriminals could use the stolen information to impersonate individuals or carry out fraud through emails, text messages and phone calls.

No hacking group has been publicly linked to the attack, and it remains unclear whether the incident involved ransomware.

Professional soccer clubs and governing bodies have become attractive targets for cybercriminals.

### ‘Grand Theft Auto VI’ hacker sentenced to seven years, released the same day
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T00:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: A British cybercriminal who hacked Rockstar Games and leaked footage of Grand Theft Auto VI was sentenced Monday to seven years in prison, and then released on license because of time served.
- Business background / implications: A British cybercriminal who hacked Rockstar Games and leaked footage of Grand Theft Auto VI was sentenced Monday to seven years in prison, and then released on license because of time served.

Arion Kurtaj, now 21, had previously been deemed unfit to stand trial by psychiatrists due to his severe autism.

In September 2022, while 17, Kurtaj broke into the systems of ride-hailing business Uber , fintech firm Revolut , and the developer of Grand Theft Auto in a spate of hacks committed while on bail for earlier offenses.

Kurtaj had been placed in a hotel for his own protection after being threatened by rival hackers. Although police had confiscated his computer, he managed to hack the companies using an Amazon Fire Stick plugged into the hotel room’s television.

He pleaded guilty last week at Woolwich Crown Court in London to six offenses, four under the Computer Misuse Act, one of fraud and one of possessing a mobile phone in prison.

Seven other charges against Kurtaj will “lie on file,” meaning prosecutors will not pursue them without a court’s permission. The charges do not count as convictions.

The judge initially set a sentence of nine years, reducing it by two years to account for the time Kurtaj had spent detained in a psychiatric hospital.

Kurtaj will serve the remainder of his sentence under supervision in the community and can be returned to prison if he breaches the conditions of his release.

“You are obviously a very talented young man,” Judge Mark Turner told him. “Don't waste those talents by spending the rest of your life in and out of prison.”

Police said they would apply for a Serious Crime Prevention Order, which allows courts to restrict a convicted offender’s activities to prevent further crimes. BBC News reported that a five-year order had already been imposed, prohibiting Kurtaj from using the messaging apps Telegram and Discord.

“Kurtaj knew what he was doing when he carried out these crimes and has finally admitted his involvement,” said Detective Inspector Mike O'Sullivan of the City of London Police.

### Hackers breach Austrian Foreign Ministry website, access diplomats' contact details
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T00:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Austria's Foreign Ministry said late last week that hackers breached one of its websites and accessed the names and official contact information of employees at its Vienna headquarters and at Austria’s diplomatic missions abroad.
- Business background / implications: Austria's Foreign Ministry said late last week that hackers breached one of its websites and accessed the names and official contact information of employees at its Vienna headquarters and at Austria’s diplomatic missions abroad.

The breach occurred between November 12 and 13 and affected a website operated by an external service provider, according to a ministry statement reported by public broadcaster ORF on Friday.

The ministry said the breach had been contained and that no personal information belonging to members of the public was compromised. The hackers have not been identified, and it remains unclear how they gained access to the website or whether they stole any information.

The ministry said it was taking the incident "very seriously" and had notified Austria's cybersecurity and data protection authorities.

A spokesperson told Recorded Future News: “For security reasons, we do not disclose details of specific technical vulnerabilities or security measures. The incident has not been attributed to any particular actor.”

The attack coincided with a global Microsoft service outage that disrupted several of the company's services for approximately 24 hours. Microsoft attributed the disruption to a software bug and infrastructure failures. Austrian authorities have not established whether the two incidents were connected.

Austria's Foreign Ministry has previously been targeted by suspected state-sponsored hackers linked to Moscow.

In late 2019 and early 2020, the ministry suffered a cyberattack that lasted several weeks and was described by Austria's National Security Council as a targeted intelligence-gathering operation. In July 2026, Vienna publicly attributed the attack to Russia.

The latest breach comes amid worsening diplomatic relations between Austria and Russia.

In October, Vienna expelled four Russian diplomats, bringing the number of Russian diplomatic personnel declared persona non grata by Austria since 2020 to 18.

Tensions escalated further last week after a Russian drone strike damaged the Austrian Embassy in Kyiv. Austria condemned the strike as "unacceptable" and "a violation of international law," and Foreign Minister Beate Meinl-Reisinger summoned Russia's ambassador in Vienna.

There is no public evidence linking Russia to the latest cyberattack. Moscow has repeatedly denied allegations of involvement in cyberattacks against European countries.

### Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T21:32:33Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The lawsuit alleges that weak safeguards around nudification were a primary selling point for Musk’s business strategy to differentiate Grok and other competing AI models. The post Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Virginia and Oregon attorneys general sued SpaceX and Elon Musk, alleging Grok allowed users to create nonconsensual deepfake nudes.

Two new lawsuits accuse SpaceX and its subsidiaries of violating state consumer and trade laws by allowing its AI tool, Grok, to create deepfake nudes of millions of people earlier this year.

Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones and Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield, both Democrats, filed the lawsuits. They allege SpaceX and owner Elon Musk repeatedly failed to block Grok’s nudification feature after it became clear the tool was being used to break the law by creating nude or defamatory images of real people. The attorneys general want a court to bar SpaceX from advertising or profiting from the tool’s capabilities, pay restitution to victims and impose civil penalties.

“Time and again, xAI and X told consumers they were being protected from abuses. That was false,” Jones said in a statement. “Instead, the tool permitted users to routinely create and circulate non-consensual intimate imagery and sexualized images, and child sexual abuse material (CSAM), causing harm to individuals and children. Companies that deploy powerful tools don’t get to ignore consumer safety and call it innovation. The law applies to everyone, and this office will hold big tech accountable when they break the law.”

The lawsuits say Grok launched when other AI companies already knew about the harms of deepfake pornography to adults and children. Many of those companies built safeguards to keep their models from generating such content. Musk, by contrast, is facing separate lawsuits from former victims of child sexual abuse material. Those plaintiffs allege Grok can easily nudify children because it was partly trained on real abuse material found online.

“Led by founder Elon Musk, Defendants chose to ignore this growing industry concern, and instead saw a market opportunity for more profits,” the lawsuit alleged. “To push its lagging product, Grok, to tens of millions of new users, it stripped out industry-standard safeguards and marketed a ‘spicy mode’ built to produce nudity.”

As CyberScoop reported last year, Musk’s public statements and decisions to maintain Grok’s capabilities were so brazen that cybersecurity and privacy lawyers predicted he would face an avalanche of criminal and civil actions . Over the past six months, lawsuits, federal and state investigations, and international regulatory scrutiny have battered the company.

The lawsu

### Indicted Kosovo credit card theft suspect extradited to US
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T18:37:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Authorities say a hard drive in the possession of Hektor Avdyli contained millions of payment card numbers. The post Indicted Kosovo credit card theft suspect extradited to US appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Hektor Avdyli, a 21-year-old from Kosovo found with 2.7 million payment card numbers, was extradited to the U.S. to face federal credit card fraud charges.

A Kosovo man accused of selling stolen credit card and personal information over the internet, who was allegedly found with millions of payment card numbers in his possession, was extradited to the United States, the Justice Department announced Monday.

In October 2025, a federal grand jury in Wisconsin returned an eight-count indictment charging Hektor Avdyli, 21 — reportedly the nephew of a former member of Kosovo’s parliament — with wire fraud, access device fraud and aggravated identity theft.

Kosovar authorities arrested him in July at the behest of the FBI and DOJ, and he’s now in Milwaukee awaiting trial.

“Unfortunately, there is nothing remotely unusual about American consumers having their identities and financial information stolen online, often by foreign actors,” said First Assistant U.S. Attorney Brad Schimel for the Eastern District of Wisconsin. “It happens countless times every day, and that theft costs American consumers billions of dollars.”

Avdyli allegedly used online criminal forums to ply his illicit trade, advertising and selling stolen credit card data and personal information to help customers commit fraud. Court documents say that he claimed to steal the information from shopping websites using malware.

As part of the operation to apprehend him, the FBI secretly bought more than 27,000 credit card numbers and related personal information from a seller. The bureau then identified Avdyli as the culprit through its investigative work.

A hard drive in his possession at the time of his arrest had 2.7 million payment card numbers on it, according to authorities.

“FBI Milwaukee worked across international borders to target the criminal actor behind a cybercrime scheme that enabled fraud against American people to include citizens of Wisconsin,” said Special-Agent-in-Charge Alan Karr of FBI Milwaukee.

### Kirsten Davies to become national cyber director nominee, Jay Clayton takes acting director job
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T16:59:03Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: After a brief stint as acting director, Davies will become the pick to succeed Sean Cairncross and National Cyber Director Clayton will do double duty. The post Kirsten Davies to become national cyber director nominee, Jay Clayton takes acting director job appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Kirsten Davies and Jay Clayton take on new roles in a federal cyber leadership shuffle following Sean Cairncross's exit as national cyber director.

President Donald Trump will nominate Kirsten Davies for permanent national cyber director, giving the acting role she just received last week for the post to Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton pending her confirmation.

A White House official told CyberScoop Monday that “following the overwhelming support Kirsten received from industry and across government after Thursday’s announcement, President Trump has decided to nominate her to serve as national cyber director.”

Davies, in the meantime, will serve as senior adviser for the Office of the National Cyber Director. The same person cannot serve in the acting role of a position while also being the nominee for it.

The move is one of several in a recent federal cyber leadership shuffle, all following the November elections that saw Democrats take control of the House and Senate.

The departure of Sean Cairncross as Trump’s national cyber director opened up his job. Davies was the chief information officer at the Department of Defense prior to receiving the acting national cyber director position, in addition to a background of holding leading cyber roles at big companies like Siemens and Estée Lauder.

Trump also nominated Palantir CISO Michael LoSapio for permanent director of the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency following the election, although multiple senators have already placed holds on his nomination that look to delay his confirmation until next year — and that’s only if he overcomes Democrats’ early poor reception to him .

The White House official touted Clayton’s cybersecurity experience as chair of the White House Super Intelligence Force, among other work.

“I’m excited to work more with Kirsten, who has the experience and capabilities needed to help America navigate our increasingly complex and powerful digital infrastructure as our next national cyber director,” Clayton said in a statement. “I look forward to working with the ONCD team to ensure Kirsten has a seamless transition into the role upon her confirmation by the Senate.”

Clayton’s office also had an acting director on loan from another agency prior to his confirmation: Bill Pulte, the director of the Federal Housing Finance Authority, was the acting director of national intelligence from June to August of this year following the departure of Tulsi Gabbard.

### CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T16:15:50Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The deal would bring device protection and network access controls under one roof as CrowdStrike seeks a larger role in how companies secure AI agents. The post CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: CrowdStrike agreed to acquire Zscaler in a $43.9 billion all-stock deal, combining endpoint and zero-trust network security to protect AI agents.

CrowdStrike said Monday it agreed to acquire Zscaler in an all-stock deal valued at about $43.9 billion, giving the cybersecurity company a network security business alongside its software for protecting devices and cloud applications.

The acquisition would bring two kinds of security under one owner: tools that monitor what happens on a device or in a cloud application, and tools that control what those systems can connect to. That distinction matters as employees, applications and artificial intelligence agents connect directly to cloud services without necessarily routing traffic through a company data center. Zscaler’s approach, known as zero trust, checks whether a user or program is authorized to access a specific resource rather than trusting it based on where it connects from.

“The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data,” CrowdStrike founder and CEO George Kurtz said in the announcement.

Daniel Bernard, CrowdStrike’s chief business officer, said in an interview that “this isn’t a shotgun wedding” and the companies’ years of working together gave them a view of how customers were already using their products to secure AI agents.

“The network really starts on the endpoint, and that’s the genesis of how we view this transaction,” Bernard said. He said CrowdStrike’s software focuses on what an agent does on the device where it runs, while Zscaler’s software sets rules for what the agent can access over the network.

“But now where the agent travels needs to be governed and controlled as well,” he said. Under one owner, CrowdStrike plans to combine device activity and network records so security teams can examine what happened on a device alongside the connections it made. It also plans to sell both sets of products under a single contract, giving it a way to reach about 8,000 Zscaler customers that do not currently buy from CrowdStrike. “We want to be the operating system of security,” Bernard said. He described a platform where security teams could monitor activity and enforce rules across devices, cloud applications and networks, with the goal of providing “consistency and ease of use in managing security.”

Bernard said other software makers would still be able to build tools that work with CrowdStrike’s platform. “This isn’t a play of saying, ‘Hey, the only vendor yo

### Dutch privacy authority urges consumers not to buy ‘pervert glasses’
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-23T15:29:49Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The Netherlands' data protection agency said smart wearables violate people's privacy, get information wrong, and can result in unsafe situations.
- Business background / implications: The Netherlands' data protection agency said smart wearables violate people's privacy, get information wrong, and can result in unsafe situations.

The Dutch Data Protection Authority is urging consumers not to buy AI-enabled camera glasses, warning that they compromise the privacy of nearby bystanders and risk the safety of their users.

The authority, whose recent statement falls just short of an outright ban, said that use of the internet-enabled and always-on glasses to record people in public is “almost always prohibited.”

Critics have dubbed these smart wearables as “pervert glasses” for their propensity to be used to video people without their permission, particularly to harass women. While some glasses have warning lights when users are recording, privacy advocates have argued that these alerts are not clear enough. Some people have even made phone apps capable of detecting nearby smart glasses wearers.

The Dutch data protection authority warned that the glasses also produce wrong results and unsafe results. In one test carried out by the Dutch authority, a pair of unnamed glasses “gave dating advice to an adult who indicated that they are interested in a minor.” The glasses also incorrectly read a medicine label and claimed to have called an ambulance when it had not.

The Dutch government’s advice follows a similar move by the Norwegian government , which in September said it was considering a ban on camera-enabled devices.

## Behavior-based detection engineering

Generic behavior hunts are maintained separately and are not attributed to a specific campaign without source evidence:
- [Suspicious PowerShell/process execution](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_suspicious_execution.kql)
- [Scheduled-task/service persistence](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_persistence_changes.kql)
- [Entra privileged role and credential changes](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/entra_privilege_changes.kql)

## Collection errors

- None.

## Analyst workflow

1. Match KEV/advisories to asset inventory, versions and internet exposure.
2. Verify exploit status, affected versions and remediation against primary vendor/CERT advisories.
3. Derive behavior hunts from documented TTPs; map ATT&CK only when supported by evidence.
4. Verify candidate IOCs against original source, freshness and local telemetry.
5. Keep queries Draft until schema, syntax, positive/negative tests and tuning are recorded.

## Limitations

- Broad public-source baseline, not exhaustive global intelligence.
- ThreatFox, OTX, GreyNoise and Shadowserver are not enabled; review credentials and provider terms before integration.
- Business discovery uses a wider 7-day window; Google News results are reported leads and require company-announcement or filing verification.
- Feed failures and quiet periods do not mean absence of threats; report timestamps are IST and machine timestamps in JSON are UTC.
